CHAPTER I
HE rosewood sofa hadn't been sold!
It stood in the window of the small, rather
dingy shop on Eighth street surrounded by an assort-
ment of undistinguished objects. The sofa had dis-
tinction. A skillful craftsman had fashioned its
graceful frame. The wood delicately carved, shone
with a dull luster. The upholstery was of damask,
almond-green, dimly patterned with apricot flowers
and small gold leaves. There was in the rosewood
sofa a blending of character and frivolity, of ex-
travagance and enduring charm. Standing in the
clutter of objects in the window, it had the air of
a gentle aristocrat reduced to common neighbors.
away.
No
Ann paused before the window, a little ashamed
of feeling so greatly relieved. She had come to the
shop, almost running, along Eighth street with the
bulky parcel from the florist's two blocks
use making excuses. No use to tell herself that it
was nearly five o'clock and she ought to be getting
home. She knew that the cause of her undignified
haste was an oddly disquieting fear that the sofa
might have been sold.
It was absurd to care so much for things, she
thought, setting the parcel on the pavement. Books
were different—and flowers. But things, glass and
china, wood and chintz, an egg-beater from Wool-
worth's-One should cultivate a lofty indifference to pos-
sessions. That was one of Father's maxims. He
didn't mean it seriously. The maxim was a brother
to the one about cheese and grapes and Italian bread.
Father used that one frequently. And yet it was
he who insisted upon an expensive dinner when there
was money enough to be recklessly gay. Father's
maxims weren't deeply rooted convictions. They
were merely his way of making light of a some-
what haphazard existence.
It had been a haphazard existence, her life and
Father's. They hadn't had a home since Mother
died when Ann was eight years old. For Ann there
had been a succession of schools. Never the same one
long. Father had moved her from school to school
as blithely as he himself had moved from one news-
paper job to another.
She had never known when to expect a change.
Father would appear, without warning, at the school
in which she happened to be enrolled at the time.
"We're going to Boston," he would say, looking
jaunty and handsome and too young to be the parent
of leggy, half-grown Ann. Or their destination
would be Kentucky, perhaps, or Chicago or New
Orleans.
Ann would be conscious of a familiar feeling of
dismay. She would make half-hearted protests.
Couldn't they wait until the end of the term? Father
had paid her tuition for the year. Wouldn't it be
better for her to take the June examinations here?
Oughtn't she to complete the new course in music
or modeling or interpretive dancing? Extras were
so expensive. Didn't Father think-?
[ 10 ]She never gave him her real reasons for wanting
to stay. She had made new curtains for her room,
perhaps. Her narcissus bulbs were about to bloom.
She liked her roommate. She was sure to have a
prominent part in the May Day pageant. Such rea-
sons had seemed too trifling to be mentioned com-
pared with a splendid new job for Father.
He had always dismissed with a gesture her half-
expressed objections.
"I'll fix the money end of it," he would say
easily. "I'll ask for a re-fund. Miss Houghton
admires me." Or Miss Russell or Miss Brecken-
ridge or any of the long list of head-mistresses.
And then, practical matters disposed of, "Think of
being in New Orleans for the Mardi-Gras!" Father
would say, his eyes shining with pleasure. "I'll buy
you a black lace mask and slippers with scarlet heels.
Besides, it will be good for your French. Your
pronunciation, young lady, leaves much to be
desired."
Or-
"Boston is interesting, Ann. You'll like the swan-
boats on the lake in the Commons and the pop-con-
certs and the Parker House. The 'Athens of
America' is supposed to have a cultural effect on
young ladies under eighteen. Couldn't you stand
a little culture, taken in very small doses?"
Or-
"The school in Kentucky looks attractive. Very
southern. Pillars and magnolia trees. They spe-
cialize in horseback riding. You should learn to ride,
Miss Lowell. It's a useful accomplishment."
Presently Father's high spirits would kindle reluc-
[11]tant enthusiasm in Ann. She would trot off to pack
her trunk and make her farewells and bestow upon
a tearful roommate treasures which couldn't be
transported. Leaving her behind was never con-
sidered. The idea never occurred either to Fa-
ther or to her. Where Father went, Ann went.
That was the unwritten law of their migratory
existence.
People, who knew the circumstances, thought it
quixotic of Father to take her about with him.
Grandmother Lowell, many times, had deplored the
unnecessary expense. Mother's relatives were of
the opinion that it would be the ruination of the
child, moving her around the country when she ought
to be settled somewhere, preferably with them, lead-
ing a "normal life."
To Ann it was very endearing. Father wanted
her near him. That was the thought which sup-
ported her through succeeding ordeals of making
new acquaintances, of adjusting herself to a new
routine. He might have left her with relatives, or
settled her in a permanent school and gone merrily
on his way.
He had never done that. He had found a school
for her near wherever he happened to be working,
except the year that he had gone, for the Geographic
Society, with an expedition to Egypt. Ten-year-old
Ann had been left in a convent-school in Washington
and though she had tried to be cheerful when Father
returned, he knew how dreadfully she had missed
him, how very unhappy she'd been all the time he
had been gone.
It hadn't happened again. After the convent epi-
[12]sode her school had been close enough for frequent
visits with Father.
So many schools! Standing beneath the small
swinging sign which announced in inconspicuous let-
ters "T. MacArdle-Antiques," Ann mentally re-
viewed them. There was the school in Maryland
when Father was working on the Baltimore Sun,
the school outside of Chicago, the convent, Miss
Houghton's near Boston, Breckenridge Manor in
Kentucky, St. Theresa's in New Orleans, Kenwood
Hall in Connecticut from which she had been gradu-
ated last June. If there was any truth in the proverb
about the rolling stone, Ann reflected, she should
have acquired a very fine polish, indeed!
She'd had the best of it, though. The schools,
when new surroundings became familiar, had been
unexcitingly alike. They had bred in her a lasting
distaste for canned apricots and cocoa, for cot-beds
and rough-plaster walls, for gym bloomers and ex-
clusively feminine society. But they had been more
comfortable, and certainly more attractive, than the
boarding-houses, the small hotels, the bachelor
"rooms" that had sheltered Father.
She'd been a great expense. Father hadn't made
very much money. Journalists didn't, Ann had ob-
served, even versatile ones like Father, unless they
were affiliated with one paper long enough to acquire
a certain prestige or did spectacular things such as
climbing the Himalayas or riding a bicycle through
the war-zone of a foe-infested country, or getting
themselves captured by Chinese bandits.
Father hadn't stayed in one place long enough to
acquire more than a budding promise of prestige
[13]and she had prevented spectacular experiences. He
couldn't, she thought with a smile, have pedaled
through a war-zone on a tandem bicycle with a
daughter perched behind.
Father made light of the matter, of course, when-
ever she spoke of expenses.
"You're my hobby," he had said on one occasion.
"I suspect that I'd have to have one. It might be
race-horses or fancy vests or 'Ladies of the En-
semble.' As a hobby you're comparatively inex-
pensive. Think what fancy vests might run to, or
'Ladies of the Ensemble.'"
Father's pleasantries amused her but they didn't
close her mind to actual facts. She knew that her
school bills, her vacations, her spending-money, her
clothes, made it necessary for him to live in board-
ing houses and small hotels. He hadn't seemed to
mind them especially. He was as adaptable as a
chameleon. But she had hated dreary quarters for
Father, petty economies, suits with two pairs of
trousers.
Well, all of that belonged to the past. Existence
need no longer be haphazard. She and Father
might live very comfortably now that he had been
made an associate editor of the magazine and she
had a job and there were no more staggering school
bills. The apartment in Washington Square might
become a permanent home.
Home! The word was important and precious
to Ann. The picture it suggested had changed as she
had changed, through succeeding stages of growing
up, from a weedy child, all legs and eyes, into the
slender twenty-year old Ann, very smart in her dark
[14]gray suit, who stood before the window of T. Mac-
Ardle's shop. And yet, she reflected, the real mean-
ing of the word hadn't greatly changed. "Home,"
to Ann at twenty, meant much the same as it had
at ten-a dwelling in which everything, from wall-
paper to kitchen knives, was wholly, entirely one's
own.
The rosewood sofa, standing serenely behind the
small diamond shaped panes of glass which formed
the window, was more than a graceful piece of fur-
niture. It was a symbol of the home she meant to
make for Father. That, after all, was the cause of
her undignified trot from the florist's. She had set
her heart on the sofa. It was to be the beginning
of a home.
Well, there it was. And she had money enough
in her purse to make a substantial first payment. The
sofa was lovely, the graceful lines, the carving, the
almond-green upholstery. She admired it with quiet
enthusiasm, hearing, meanwhile, only half con-
sciously, the music of a hand-organ playing some-
where in the distance-
"Though April showers
May come this way—'
"
Ann supplied the familiar words, humming lightly
under her breath. The melody, muted by the con-
tinuous noise of the city, sounded thin and tinkling,
like a music-box-
"So don't be sighing
Have no regrets,
It isn't raining rain, you know,
It's raining violets-"
[15]Perhaps it was because it was spring that she
wanted to acquire possessions, to begin the delight-
ful task of making a home. During the winter she
hadn't minded, especially, the furnishings they had
rented with the apartment. Well, spring was nest-
making time.
Nest-making time! Ann's nose crinkled with
amusement. It was ridiculous to think of Father in
anything as small and cosy and confining as the word
nest implied, Father with his varying enthusiasms,
his vagabond instincts, his propensity for collecting
acquaintances. Besides, nests were impermanent.
She wanted a permanent home.
Nest-making was a pretty fancy, perhaps, but it
had nothing to do with her. More likely it was a
passion for spring cleaning, inherited from Grand-
mother Lowell, which had spurred her into wang-
ling new paper and paint from Miss Kate and Miss
Eloise, which made it seem vitally necessary to dis-
card the shabby davenport and install the rosewood
sofa.
No, it wasn't that. Ann brushed away the sug-
gestion. She wasn't particularly domestic. She had
no thwarted longings to beat up omelettes or polish
silver or bake fluffy biscuits, though she was surpris-
ingly efficient at household chores. She did have,
however, a deep longing to make Father com-
fortable.
He liked the apartment in Washington Square.
It had given her an odd sort of happiness to see him
sitting in an easy chair beside the fire, his feet on the
low brass fender, contentedly smoking his pipe. He
liked the small, and very informal dinners, which
[16]she, with Magnolia's assistance, gave occasionally
to entertain his friends.
He liked, even better, parties which hadn't been
arranged. Father was at his best when people came
in unexpectedly and others were summoned by tele-
phone and there was a great deal of happy confu-
sion, talk and music, nonsensical games, the guests
running out and in to supplement the deficiencies of
the ice box. It was after a particularly gay party
that Father had said:
"I think I'll stay on with the magazine. They've
offered to make me an associate editor. I like all
this." His eyes had moved about the long, high-
ceilinged room, bright with firelight and the soft
glow of shaded lamp. "I must have domestic
instincts."
Bless him! Ann's heart swelled with tenderness.
Existence need no longer be haphazard. She and
Father would travel, of course. He would never be
content to stay in the same place always. Neither,
for that matter, would she. There were many places
they wanted to visit. But they would leave behind
them a home, to be remembered with affection while
they were travelling, to welcome them when they
returned.
Home! It was an important word. She was so
weary of schools and camps and boarding houses and
small hotels. She wanted her own bed, her own
rugs to turn back for dancing. She wanted to pow-
der her nose before the mirror of her own dressing-
table. She wanted her own chairs and bookshelves,
her own pots and pans in the kitchenette. She
wanted the rosewood sofa.
[17]Well, she might have it very soon. She could
make a payment today. Mr. MacArdle would keep
it for her until she could pay the balance.
He
would doubtlessly be relieved. She had stopped in
to consult him about the sofa at least half a dozen
times since she had discovered it less than a month
ago.
She liked Mr. MacArdle. She liked to hear him
talk. There was a burr in his deep rumbling voice.
He showed her his treasures, occasional lovely
things in the clutter of odds and ends. Her calls at
the shop usually lengthened into visits. She often
suspected that she had, to a lesser degree, Father's
gift for collecting odd acquaintances.
She would have the rosewood sofa! Ann lifted
the parcel from the pavement.
It felt insecure.
She glanced down and saw a puddle, rain water from
the recent shower. While she had been wool-gath-
ering, the parcel had been soaking. She would ask
Mr. MacArdle to give her another box.
Holding the parcel gingerly, she walked up the
steps to the door of the shop, hesitated, her hand on
the wrought-iron latch.
The hand-organ had reached her. It stood before
the shop. The musician, a bedraggled Italian with
a red bandanna knotted around his throat, was about
to release a tune. "Home Sweet Home" would be
appropriate, Ann thought, her smile deepening. A
very touching tribute.
The organ filled the air with a sudden burst of
sound. What was the melody? Certainly not
"Home Sweet Home." She thought, all at once, of
Saturday night dances at the camp in Maine-two,
[18]no three, summers ago.
escaped her.
What was it? It had
And then she remembered. Her smile faded.
She was conscious of a feeling of apprehension.
That was absurd. She smiled again. It was merely
an amusing coincidence, something to tell Father at
dinner tonight.
The hand-organ was playing-
"Blackbird, 'bye 'bye!"
[19]CHAPTER II
A DIM light filled the shop, waning daylight blend-
ing not very successfully with the glow of the
electric bulbs in four antique lanterns. Shadows
were thick in the corners, blurring the outlines of
chests and tables and tall deep-voiced clocks. Mr.
MacArdle's cat, a huge tortoise-shell, lay curled on
a cushion in the seat of a high-backed chair. There
was no one in sight.
Ann stood irresolutely in the cleared central por-
tion of the shop. Someone must be close at hand,
she thought. How could she announce her pres-
ence? The cat, blinking at her with sleepy topaz
eyes, offered no helpful suggestions.
The parcel solved the problem. Suddenly, with-
out any warning, the bottom of the box collapsed,
sending down on the floor small pots of geraniums
and ivy and pansies and sweet alyssum. The cat,
alarmed at the crash, sprang down from the chair,
its back arching, its tail militantly erect.
Ann's cheeks grew pink with embarassment and
vexation. She stood looking down at the tumbled
pots, one hand still holding by a loop of loosened
cord the soggy remains of the box. Well, she
thought, that ought to rouse somebody.
The crash, apparently, had served that useful pur-
pose. Footsteps sounded behind the partition at the
rear of the shop. A light bloomed directly aboveAnn's head, a sudden radiance from a ceiling bulb
in a circular shade. It was a little disconcerting.
Fortunately, Mr. MacArdle wasn't entirely a
stranger..
But it wasn't Mr. MacArdle who came out
through the door in the rear partition. It was a
young man Ann hadn't seen before, a slim sandy
brown young man, not very much taller than she.
"Well, well!" he said, apparently
said, apparently greatly
amused. His eyes travelled down from the brim.
of her hat to the small pots of flowers scattered about
her feet. "You look like a cover for the Saturday
Evening Post," he continued. "Date-any week in
spring."
"May I speak to Mr. MacArdle ?" Ann asked in
a voice which she hoped sounded crushing and cool.
The chilly tones were an affectation. Actually she
was struggling against a desire to laugh.
"That was fresh, wasn't it?" The strange young
man assumed a contrite expression.
"Very fresh," Ann agreed. The laugh gave her
a difficult moment. The strange young man looked
so debonaire and, in spite of the contrite expression,
neither crushed nor repentant.
"I'm sorry. No, I'm not." The contrite expres-
sion vanished. His eyes were blue, Ann discovered,
and his smile was very engaging. "You did look
like a cover for the Post. A human interest idea,
you know, the pots of flowers tumbled out of the
box, your half smile. The artist would have spoiled
it, though. He'd have dressed you in red to attract
attention, instead of that nice-looking-" He broke
off abruptly and again Ann saw that his smile was
[21]very engaging. "I seem to be getting fresh again,"
he said after a moment. "You wanted to see Mr.
MacArdle?"
"Yes. Isn't he here?"
"No. I'm keeping shop. With Taffy's assist-
ance." He indicated the tortoise-shell cat, curled
on its cushion again in the high-backed chair. "Can't
we do something for you?"
"You can get me a box, if it isn't too much
trouble."
"Certainly." He took the remains of the box she
held and went out through the door in the rear par-
tition, saying as he disappeared, "I'll see what I can
find."
When he had gone, Ann walked to a long mirror
set in a swinging frame of gilt. Who was he, she
wondered, stripping off her gloves. She absently
tilted the brim of her hat, tucked a hairpin into the
knot at the nape of her neck. Was he Mr. Mac-
Ardle's son? She couldn't think that he was. And
yet he seemed thoroughly familiar with the shop.
It was a nice-looking suit. It emphasized her best
features, her clear dark-lashed gray eyes, widely
spaced under straight dark brows, her erect carriage,
her long graceful legs. She wasn't pretty, she re-
flected, but she did have her points. Heavens !
Was that a run in her stocking?
No, he wasn't gruff old Mr. MacArdle's son, Ann
decided, when she assured herself that the sheer
stocking was flawless. He looked too expensive to
be intimately related to the shop. His tweed suit
had been made for him by a tailor who knew his
business. His pale blue shirt and dark blue tie were
[22]the kind she could afford to buy for Father only on
special occasions. His shoes had the rich mahogany
tones she liked.
How brown he was! Bending toward the mirror,
Ann carefully reddened her lips. Tans like his, she
thought, weren't the product of the early spring sun
in New York. They were acquired in Florida or
Cuba or Bermuda. He had the manner, too, of a
young man who found life effortless and pleasant, a
debonaire assurance, an easy casual charm. What
could be his connection with Mr. MacArdle's shop?
He must not find her standing before the mirror.
Her position was already sufficiently humiliating
without adding the suggestion of prinking for the
purpose of making an impression. She moved away
from the mirror. What was he doing? He was
taking a very long time. . .
The young man presently appeared with a basket.
"I can't find a box," he said apologetically. "Do
you mind carrying a basket?"
"Not at all," Ann assured him. "I'll take a taxi
from here."
"It's more reliable, any way." He went to the
place beneath the light where the small pots lay
scattered.
"I'll return the basket," Ann said.
"Don't bother," he said amiably. "You may keep
it—a souvenir from the management."
He knelt to collect the pots. Ann knelt at the
same moment. Their heads bumped smartly.
"Sorry!" they said simultaneously, and then, set-
tling back on their heels, they began to laugh.
"A sister act," said the strange young man.
[ 23 ]"And," Ann added, "we've never had a re-
hearsal."
They laughed again. Together they placed the
pots in the basket.
"These bronze colored pansies are nice," he said.
"Aren't they?" Ann agreed.
"What are these white things?"
"Sweet alyssum."
"And this green and white vine?"
"Snow-on-the-mountain."
"You're well informed."
"I just bought them."
He had a pleasant voice, Ann thought-inter-
ested, alive. His eyes were a very bright blue. That
was because his skin was so brown, perhaps. There
was a faint suggestion of a ripple in his light reddish-
brown hair, though it was brushed flat and smooth.
What was his name? It ought to be "Sandy." She
tried it experimentally under her breath. "Sandy."
Yes, it suited him.
"Why didn't you leave the pots at the florist's?"
he asked presently.
"I like them."
"That's a very good reason. Well, why didn't
you have them sent?"
"This is Saturday afternoon. I want to plant
them tomorrow."
"Bone-meal is good for gardens."
"For roof-gardens?" Ann asked.
"Oh!" he smiled.
"A very small one.
"It's a roof-garden, is it?"
There'll be just room enough
for two flower-boxes and two canvas chairs."
"Two? Good! I'll reserve one for Saturday
[24]nights. I'm getting fresh again. You seem to have
that effect on me. I think it's because your nose
tilts at the tip."
"Does it?"
"Hadn't you noticed?"
She sighed. "I'd hoped I was mistaken."
"I like it. It gives your face an adventurous ex-
pression. It's my favorite kind of nose."
There seemed to be no answer to that.
They
were silent for a moment. Then both spoke at once.
"Have you ?”
you-?"
"Did you ever-?"
"What were you going to say?"
"What were you?"
"I can't remember."
"That's funny. Neither can I."
They laughed again. The shop seemed filled with
laughter; filled, too, with a sweet disturbing magic.
Their hands, placing in the basket the small pots
of flowers and vines, moved slowly, lingeringly.
A clock struck six.

CHAPTER III
HE rosewood sofa stood under the light in the
center of the shop.
"It's a nice one," said the strange young man,
dusting his hands, pulling his coat into place. He
had had some difficulty removing the sofa from the
window. His expression indicated modest pride in
the achievement.
"I fell in love with it a month ago." Ann's fingers
moved caressingly along the wood of the frame.
"Let's try out the springs," he suggested.
They sat on the rosewood sofa. He turned to
her with a polite, distant expression.
"Nice weather we're having," he said in the man-
ner of a young man making a formal call.
"Lovely," Ann agreed. "Cream or lemon?" she
asked, continuing the game.
"I never drink tea. It gives me the jaundice.
Are you fond of reading, Miss Imogene ?"
"Oh, yes." Demurely she quoted—
"There is no frigate like a book
To bear us lands away—'
"That's Christine Rossetti, isn't it?"
Ann lowered her lashes, folded her hands in her
lap.
"I feel that it's taking a liberty for me to pre-sume to correct a young gentleman of your superior
attainments," she said in a small prim voice, "but
I must inform you that the quotation is from a poem
by Emily Dickinson."
He frowned, drawing his brows down over his
eyes.
"You're a blue-stocking, Miss Imogene."
"Oh, no, Mr. Carruthers," she protested. "It's
only that Mamma has a taste for poetry. I read
to her when she suffers from a migrain. It seems to
soothe her, poor darling."
He laughed, ending the scene.
"You're good," he said. "I can see the side-curls
and the maidenly blush."
"I've just re-read Pride and Prejudice."
"You are a blue-stocking."
"Oh, no, really I'm not," she protested very
earnestly.
They talked of books they had read, of plays they
had seen. He was a delightful companion, Ann
thought, hearing through flitting fancies the pleasant
tones of his voice. He had-what was it? Mag-
netism, vitality, charm, the quality without an exact
definition which drew you to some people as you
were drawn to a crackling fire on a hearth. Who
was he? "Sandy," she said softly, under her
breath...
The clock struck one deep note. Ann roused with
a start.
"I must go," she said. "It's half-past something."
"Please," his hand touched her arm.
"I must." Her attention turned to the business
of purchasing the sofa. "I want to pay a deposit
[ 27 ]on this." She opened her purse. "Can I give the
money to you?"
"Oh, yes," he assured her. "I'm thoroughly re-
liable."
"I'll pay the balance in a week or two. Mr. Mac-
Ardle will keep the sofa for me."
He took the small roll of bills. "I must give you
a receipt," he said. "And take your name and ad-
dress." He left the sofa, returned presently with
a pencil and pad. "The name?" he asked. His
manner was briskly business-like but his eyes
twinkled.
"Ann Lowell," she replied.
"An-n-without an e?" he asked.
She nodded.
"It's like you," he said.
"Plain?"
"Simple," he amended. "No superfluous frills.
The address?" he continued.
Ann gave him the number of the house on Wash-
ington Square.
"Phone number?"
"Is that necessary?"
"Oh, yes," he answered gravely. "A rule of the
management."
She supplied the information.
"Age?" he presently asked.
"Mine?" Her gray eyes widened in surprise.
"Is that a rule of the management, too?"
"It isn't important. Let it pass.
continued.
"Cafe au lait," Ann replied, laughing.
He didn't agree.
Color?" he
[ 28 ]"Light olive," he pronounced. "With an under-
tone of peach."
"You make it sound attractive."
"It is," he assured her. "Eyes? I'll answer that
one. Gray, with a dark ring around the iris," he
wrote, or pretended to write, repeating the words
aloud. "And very superlative lashes."
"Idiot!"
"Occupation?" he resumed.
"Typist."
"Really?" He seemed surprised.
"I work in a typing agency," she said.
"Why?" he asked. "It sounds awfully dull."
She didn't give him the true reason. That was
a secret which she shared with Father. Father was
going to write a novel and she was to be his sec-
retary. He probably never would, she thought. He
hadn't mentioned it for more than a year. That was
the reason, however. That was why she had taken
a course in typing at a business school last winter.
She couldn't tell this amusing young man of Fa-
ther's secret ambition.
"It's educational," she answered, instead. "I'm
doing a paper on Geology now for a professor at
Columbia. Did you know that there is a glacier in
Alaska more than fifty miles long? I've really
learned a great deal."
"You astound me!" The pencil, held in his lean

CHAPTER IV
NN opened the front door of the house in
Washington Square, closed it behind her. The
vestibule was lit by electric bulbs in a drooping clus-
ter of small frosted-glass grapes.
"The Temple of Bacchus," Father had said when
they had come to see the apartment last fall.
They had discovered, however, that there was
nothing even remotely Bacchanalian in the estab-
lishment conducted by Miss Kate and Miss Eloise
Vanden, spinster sisters, the last surviving members
of an old and formerly well-to-do family. The high
narrow brick house held an atmosphere of quiet dig-
nity, of immaculate cleanliness, of order meticulously
preserved.
Miss Kate, the elder sister, was the genius behind
the prevailing cleanliness. To Miss Kate the con-
tinual warfare against dirt and disorder was en-
tirely a labor of love. She kept the house as nearly
as possible as it had been kept in days of affluence
so that Miss Eloise might not feel too sharply the
decline of their fortunes.
"I don't mind for myself," Miss Kate had con-
fided to Ann at the close of one exhausting day.
"I'd be contented in two rooms somewhere if they
were clean and comfortable. But Sister is different.
She seems to mind things so. It would kill her if
we should have to give up the house."So, while Miss Eloise read sentimental novels on
a Chippendale sofa in the first floor apartment, or
tended her faded prettiness at a toilet table ruffled
and flounced like a bassinet, or went out to make
little formal calls, Miss Kate toiled early and late,
oiling the household machinery, exacting prodigious
service from Simpson, the caretaker, and Ida, his
plump, phlegmatic young wife.
Miss Kate's genius achieved more profitable re-
sults than the thorough spoiling of Miss Eloise. It
made the house a pleasant place in which to live.
The rental price of an apartment included a lease
on Miss Kate's sympathy and interest, her inex-
haustible energy. She pampered her tenants in a
kindly way that was neither smothering nor offi-
cious. She was always at hand when she was needed,
stout, well past fifty, a little short of breath, with a
round high-colored face, a saving streak of shrewd-
ness and the kindest heart in the world.
Usually, Ann was conscious of the atmosphere of
the house as soon as she opened the door. Each
home-coming was a link in the chain of affection
which bound her to Miss Kate's establishment. To-
night, however, the spotless white paint of the ves-
tibule, the shining brass, the diffused light of the
frosted-glass grapes, were merely a part of the
felicity of the day. The pleasure of coming home
merged with the sense of happiness and high adven-
ture which made her feel as though her trim kid
slippers were treading air.
It had been a happy day, she thought, setting the
basket of plants on the floor, searching through her
purse for the key to the mail-box. She had ac-
[ 33 ]quired a part interest in the rosewood sofa. She had
had an amusing encounter with a very engaging

Jonathan! Ann smiled as she opened the door

him Jonathan, she was sure, since Grandmother
Lowell died, except when she herself called him that
in a moment of teasing gaiety. He was "Jon" or
"Jonny" to his friends. They had other names for
him, too. Father seemed to inspire in his friends
a sturdy masculine affection which they expressed in
mildly abusive nicknames.
In the hall Ann was fleetingly conscious of the
characteristic odor of the house, brass polish and
floor wax, the damp newspapers and tea leaves with
which the rugs were periodically cleaned, blending
with the fragrance of the flowers in the bowl on the
table beneath the oval mirror. The flowers, to-

her head and saw her reflection in the mirror. She
looked unusually well, she thought. Her cheeks
[ 34 ]were flushed, her eyes were bright. The yellow
blouse was nice with the dark gray suit. Her nose
did tilt a little at the tip.
"It gives your face an adventurous expression,"
the strange young man had said. "It's my favorite
kind of nose."
His voice had given the words significance. His
manner was subtly flattering, she thought, turning
to climb the long flight of stairs to the second floor.
She had felt quite as attractive as his manner had
implied that she was. That gift for subtle flattery
was rare. Few people had it. Father did. . . .
The thought, coming unexpectedly, halted Ann's
progress up the stairs. The young man in the shop
was like Father. Strange that the similarity hadn't
occurred to her before. In appearance they weren't
at all alike. Temperamentally they were. Father,
too, had the gift for making quite ordinary people
feel brilliant or beautiful or distinguished. The
young man in the shop had Father's facility for
light conversation. That was why she had felt so
entirely at ease. She had always said that if she ever
found anyone like Father. . . .
He was coming to see her, coming to reclaim the
basket. She had liked him at once. And he had
been attracted to her. Or was that only his casual
way with ladies in a predicament? She didn't be-
lieve that quite. He had asked her to have dinner
with him. He had prolonged their parting, leaning
in through the taxi window-
"I'm absent-minded. Pansies are for thoughts,
you know."
He had tucked the bronze-colored pansy into his
[ 35 ]coat lapel. The color was right for him. He was
slim and sandy and brown. Why hadn't she asked
his name? It hadn't seemed to matter. She had a
name for him. Sandy. Did anyone call him that?
He was coming to see her. The thought lent wings
to her feet. She felt them lifting, bearing her swiftly
up the stairs. . . .
Ann paused in the second-floor hall, arrested by

The room had been transformed into a banquet-

[36]"Where have you been?" That was Father. He

Presently she took the basket of flowers into the

[ 40 ]CHAPTER V
ATHER's dinner was very gay. It was likely to
be very indigestible as well, Ann thought, sur
veying from her place at the head of the table the
strange assortment of food which Father had pro-
vided for his guests. He had evidently looted his
favorite delicatessen. That cake! Three tiers of
it covered with pink and white sugar icing! It was
probably stale inside. How could they dispose of
it? Well, Magnolia would be glad to take it home
to her hungry brood in Harlem.
Father had bought the cake because he thought it
looked festive, of course. She knew just how his
eyes had twinkled when he told the shop-keeper to
wrap it up. Or perhaps he had directed a dazed and
bewildered clerk to put it in a band-box. Father
delighted in bewildering shop-keepers and waitresses
and clerks and hat-check girls. There was nothing
in his appearance to prepare them for his amazing
remarks. He looked distinguished even when, ac-
tually, he was shabby. He could assume, on occa-
sions, a very impressive manner. That evening at
the Roosevelt last winter when they were celebrat-
ing his promotion- What was it Father had said?
She couldn't remember. But she did recall the
waiter's expression. He had looked as astonished
as though a visiting ambassador had pulled a rab-
bit from the pocket of his frock coat.Father had a definite flair for dramatics, Ann
thought, making an occasional reply but only half
hearing the remarks directed to her by Miss Kate,
at her left, and Tommy Allen, at her right. It was
so like him to have given up his job five minutes after
his legacy arrived, so like him to have burned his
bridges behind him with one flippant gesture.
And yet the party was not merely a gesture. Fa-
ther was genuinely fond of the people who lived in
the house. He wanted them to help him celebrate
his long-deferred good fortune. Father was loyal
to his friends. He was always turning up at the
apartment with somebody or other who had worked
on the paper with him in Louisville or Boston or
Chicago or New Orleans. More often than not
these friends from Father's roving past looked seedy
and down-at-the-heels. That never mattered to
Father. He welcomed them with enthusiasm, fed
them, sometimes kept them overnight.
Father's loyalty to friends and chance acquaint-
ances was splendid, of course. It could be an-
noying as well. Ann recalled the afternoon last fall
when she had been entertaining Phyllis Starr, an
elegant young lady whom she had known at Miss
Houghton's, the school outside of Boston. Father
had appeared with that dreadful Travers man from
Baltimore, so loud and rough, with racey looking
clothes and the manners of a dancing bear. Phyllis
hadn't called again. Not that it had mattered
especially. . . .
"A penny for your thoughts," Tommy Allen said,
bringing Ann's attention back to the party.
"Only a penny?" Ann turned to Tommy, a ro-
[ 42 ]tund young man with a round good-natured face and
round near-sighted brown eyes blinking through the
lenses of shell-rimmed spectacles. Tommy was
Miss Kate's nephew. He worked for an importing
company and was Ann's most faithful admirer.
"That wasn't very bright, was it?" Tommy said
amiably. "What I meant was, why don't you pay
some attention to me?"
"Are you in need of attention?" Tommy, Ann
observed, had gotten himself up for the occasion.
He wore a flannel suit, obviously new, and a college
necktie, gaudy with alternate stripes of crimson and
gold. Tommy should have selected a college with
more subdued colors. The crimson and gold ac-
cented, rather than subdued, his rubicund com-
plexion.
"I thought it would be polite to tell you that this
is a nice party." Tommy heroically attacked a sec-
ond helping of delicatessen turkey and potato
salad.
"It's Father's party," Ann said. "I hadn't any-
thing to do with it."
"Your father is one swell hombre," Tommy said
feelingly. "He's a grand person, Ann."
He was a grand person. Ann looked through the
candle-light at Father, seated opposite her, at the
other end of the table. Father was at his best to-
night. How young he looked! The gray in his
hair seemed theatrical, as though he were made up
to play an elderly rôle on the stage. He had an
olive complexion, darker than her own, and his teeth
were very white.
Ann watched him, a tender expression touching
[ 43 ]her wide gray eyes. Father was enjoying his party.
His lean dark face was lighted, at intervals, by a
flashing smile as he talked to Holly, at his left, to
Miss Eloise, at his right. It was so like him to have
worn evening clothes. She hadn't straightened his
tie. Holly was doing it for her. Holly adored
Father....
"So you're going away," Miss Kate said, inter-
rupting Ann's thoughts.
They were going away! She had forgotten it
for a moment.
"Yes," she said.
"Your father told me." Miss Kate spoke in a
cautious whisper. Evidently, Ann inferred, Father
hadn't announced their approaching departure to
the company at large.
"We hate to lose you," Miss Kate continued.
Ann looked at Miss Kate, stout and rosy, wear-
ing her best black silk and her crystal beads, her
thin graying hair crimped for the party. Miss
Kate had been kind when she was ill last winter.
She had brought her broth and custards and jelly
molded in fancy shapes. She was in and out of the
apartment all day while Father was at the office.
"Will you?" she asked.

"He'll survive," Ann said lightly, surprised and

[ 44 ]Tommy was rather a dear, she thought. He was so
openly, so patiently devoted to her. He brought
her little gifts from the importing house, packets
of tea done up in gilt and scarlet paper, animals
carved from soap-stone and wood, a Japanese doll,
boxes of candied ginger.
She remembered an excursion she had taken with
Tommy. They had gone on the ferry to Staten
Island and back one clear cold day last January, had
walked along the Battery, visited the Aquarium.
Tommy had been fun that day, buying her peanuts
and popcorn, making her wear his heavy gloves over
her own, sharing her interest in the penguins and
seals, in the rainbow-colored fish, swishing their
gauzy fins through the greenish water.
He had brought her a bowl of gold-fish to immor-
talize the excursion. The gold-fish had been sadly
mortal. They had died the week-end that she and
Father had gone to Lake Placid and something had
happened to the heating plant and the temperature
of the apartment had been below freezing when they
returned. Smiling, she thought of how she had
hurried out the first thing the next morning to re-
place the fish so that Tommy's feelings wouldn't be
hurt.
Ann reined in her straying thoughts. It was high
time, she decided. She was actually getting emo-
tional about Tommy Allen. The imminence of go-
ing away was making her absurdly sentimental.
She was forgetting the many times that Tommy's
heavy wit, his leaning toward practical jokes, his
tediously retold anecdotes, had bored her to the
point of exhaustion. She was forgetting the count-
[ 45 ]less reasons she had invented for declining his per-
sistent invitations.
She didn't want to go away. That was why she
felt emotional. Tommy was a part of the life which
had become familiar and pleasant. She would miss
her friends in the old brick house. She felt closer
to them than to any group of people she had known.
She was fond of both Holly and her mother, Nina.
She
Ann glanced at Nina, seated half-way along the

was small and very thin. Her short amber-colored

Nina had had an adventurous life. She was born
in England and had been, in her early youth, an
actress on the English stage. Later she had come
to America with her husband, Geoffrey Kent. They
had toured in Shakespearean repertory and with
companies producing English plays. The death of
her husband, when Holly was sixteen, had ended
Nina's career, except for occasional returns to the
stage when an enterprising producer revived an old
favorite to add variety to a season's theatrical fare.
Ann had wondered, during the progress of her
first call in Nina's and Holly's apartment, how they
had contrived to break through Miss Kate's Vic-

theater.
"Shakespeare sounds so respectable," Nina had

[46]later. "Holly had taken a fancy to the apartment
and so I carefully concealed the fact that I had just
finished playing the gambler's wife in 'Ten Nights

Once they were established in the basement apart-

Holly as her own. She would have been the first
to resent any implication against their thorough re-
spectability. Ann, waiting in the hall for Father

Geoffrey Kent, the actor, in Miss Kate's saga, mi-

"I guess that will hold her!" Miss Kate had said

"I'll have no questions about the people who live in

I never could abide her."
Nina was splendid company. She had an endless

Jerome Gordon was smiling. That was a tribute

had a small blonde mustache and a fancy for pastel

was shy, almost embarassingly polite. No one, ex-
[ 47 ]cept Father, Ann was sure, ever called him "Jerry."
Music was Mr. Gordon's passion. He had, in

She had gone with him to the opera one evening

had touched her, but the effort of putting him at ease
had been too fatiguing for a repetition. There was
one person, at least, Ann thought, whom she would

"I'm not asleep," she answered, returning Holly's

"I'm not sufficiently entertaining." Tommy drew

A laugh rippled around the table.
"That reminds me-" Nina began, in the low,
slightly husky voice which gave her English accent
a droll, amusing quality. Presently she slipped into
the cockney dialect she did so well.
The party gave Nina its collective attention. Ann,
listening absently, sat looking at Holly. It would
[ 48 ]be harder to leave Holly than any of the others, she

courage, enjoyed her companionship.
Holly was older than she-twenty-four. It was
hard for strangers to believe that she was Nina's.
daughter. She was tall and very slender with an ap-
pealing sort of awkward grace. Her heavy dark
hair was wound in coronet braids around her small,
well-shaped head. She had a pale skin, dark eyes,
a wide mouth with full lips reddened to the deep
crimson of the oddly cut frock she wore. Her hands
were beautiful. They expressed, unconsciously, her
thoughts, her varying moods.
Holly was the typical stage child of romantic fic-
tion. She had made her first appearance behind foot-
lights as the infant in "East Lynn." She had grown
up in schools and convents and theatrical boarding-
houses. Since her father had died, when she was
sixteen, she had supported Nina with the assistance
of a small income from a trust fund established by
her husband.
Holly's ambition was to be an actress. That am-
bition was the most important thing in her life. She
had played with summer stock companies and, oc-
casionally, had a small part in New York. Between
engagements she accepted any job which would add
to her income and leave her time to make the rounds
of theatrical agencies. At present she had a part in
a radio skit depicting the ups and down in the first
year of married life of a fictitious "Betty and Bob."
Men were attracted to Holly. She treated them
with a casual indifference, having, as she expressed
it, neither time nor energy for amorous interludes.
[49]She worked very hard. Nothing which might help
her to realize her ambition was neglected. She took
voice lessons, when the state of her finances war-
ranted the expense, lessons in French and Italian, in
fencing and dancing.
Holly could be a great actress, Ann thought. She
had patience, vitality, a capacity for work. Her
voice was flexible, moving. She was the first real
friend Ann had had. There were girls in the various
schools whom she had liked well enough, but they
had seemed childish and immature. Holly was ma-
ture. She had a stimulating philosophy of life. She
was good fun, too. It would be hard to leave
Holly. . . .
"You needn't worry about the apartment," Miss

Nina's story. "I think I know someone who will
take it at once."
Miss Kate's well-meant remark made going away
seem real and close at hand. Why must they go?
She would talk to Father-
"Do you?" she asked, finding words a little
difficult.
"A young married couple," Miss Kate continued.
"The boy is a relative of a friend of mine. No
children. They've been married only a year. He
works in a bank."
Someone else would live in the apartment! A
young married couple who worked in a bank. No,
the boy worked in a bank. The wife would probably
devote her time to doing dreadful things to the
apartment. Ann's eyes moved around the room.
The new paper was nice, she thought, a dull silver-
[50]green, between panels of ivory-painted wood. It
was the right background for the rosewood sofa.
She'd have window draperies of the apricot shade
in the upholstery of the sofa. Venetian blinds, some-
time-
But she wasn't to have the rosewood sofa. Ob-
viously, they couldn't take it with them to Cape Cod
or Maine or Nova Scotia. Mr. MacArdle would
have to keep it. She and Father were going away.
Why must they? Couldn't Father write a book
here? She was old enough, now, to be consulted.
Father shouldn't have disregarded her so com-
pletely. She would talk to him. Tonight. . .
Her determination to protest against leaving
New York strengthened as the dinner progressed.
While she talked she assembled an array of for-
midable facts. Father had never tried to write.
fiction, except blood-and-thunder tales under an
assumed name for the pulp magazines when he was
hard pressed for money. Wouldn't it be wiser to
keep his job and make the experiment here? If he
failed-wouldn't it be better-?
Magnolia cleared the table, brought wine glasses
on a tray. Ann prayed that Magnolia wouldn't
drop the tray. The glasses belonged to Miss Kate.
Father had borrowed things from every apartment
in the house. A glass was set safely at each place.
Ann breathed a sigh of relief.
While Father drew the corks from the bottles of
home-made wine which Tony, the barber, had given
him, Ann continued to marshall unassailable facts.
Magnolia poured the sherry-colored liquid into the
glasses, like irridescent bubbles lifted on slender
[51]stems. . . . If Father failed, she thought, it would
mean looking for another job, worries about money,
boarding-houses. She wasn't a child. .
"Friends, Romans, Country-men!" Tommy
boomed, scattering Ann's valiant army of facts.
"Pipe down!" Holly called.
Ann glanced at the opposite end of the table. Fa-
ther was about to make a speech. Urged by Holly,
he rose, stood with one hand resting on the back of
his chair. He would tell them now, Ann thought.
He would tell them they were going away.
Usually Father's speeches were amusing. He
wasn't at top form tonight. Speaking simply, he
told his guests that he and Ann were leaving New
York. He was vague about the reason. Work, he
said, something he wanted to do. His voice wasn't
quite steady. His smile was a little crooked. Fa-
ther, Ann thought, was feeling emotional, too.
A flurry of exclamations, of questions, followed
Father's valedictory.
"Gee, Ann! Gosh!" Tommy's round face wore
an expression of blank dismay. "Where are you
going?" he asked.
"I don't know."
"Sudden, isn't it?"
"Rather."
"Gee!" Then Tommy was on his feet. "We
ought to have a toast," he said, his voice too cheer-
ful, too loud.
The guests rose, stood looking at Tommy. Ann
remained seated.
Tommy lifted his glass.
"Here's to " he began bravely, then came to an
[52]abrupt stop, floundering on the shoals of an
certain memory. "Here's to-" He paused again.
"I can't remember it," he confessed, a rueful expres-
sion clouding his face. "Well, anyway," he con-
tinued, bowing gallantly to Ann, to Father, "Well,
anyway-here's to luck!"
Ann looked at Father, seated at the other end of
the table. He was looking at her. His expression
was questioning, oddly grave, a little apprehensive.
She was suddenly ashamed. Dear Father! Smil-
ing, she touched her lips to the rim of her glass.
But she wasn't a child.
Tonight...
She would talk to him.

CHAPTER VI
NN tied the cord of a quilted dressing gown,

They had never owned an automobile. Ann was

She asked the question.
"I can learn, can't I?" His voice sounded a little

It's really very simple. You step on the starter and

"Maybe" Ann said.
He disregarded, for the moment, her obvious lack

[55]"Rents aren't high. We can get a house on the

"Ugh!" Ann grimaced.

She did not answer at once. She sat looking down
at the maps on the table. Here was the opportunity

What were her reasons? She liked the apartment.
She had bought the rosewood sofa. She had met a
very engaging young man. The reasons seemed in-

for Father to write a novel, as her reasons had been
for wanting to stay at Miss Houghton's or Breck-

of her early, inarticulate youth, she resorted to

"Couldn't you do it here?" she asked, not look-

write at odd times-evenings, Sundays?"
"I can't work that way, Ann," Father's voice was
serious. "It's something lacking in me. I haven't
sufficient concentration, detachment - whatever it
[56]takes." He moved restlessly in his chair. "I'm fed
up with the job. I need to get away.'
"I thought you liked the magazine."
"Oh, it's a good job. A worthy occupation," he

and bridge prizes and pecan pie.'

"You don't do that part of it."
"I have," he confessed, "in an emergency. Well,

When he spoke again his voice was grave. "I'm

"But if I helped you, Father," Ann said earnestly,

"I like people. It's an affliction."

[57]pressure for years. Always rushing, always a dead-

A silence followed the low hesitant question. Ann
sat motionless, ashamed and repentant. An imp of
perversity had prompted the question, an imp born
of disappointment and apprehension, an imp which
had something to do with a fleeting but vivid mem-

She waited for Father's reply.
99
It was mild enough when it came. "In that case,'

But she knew that the question troubled him. Al-

"You're tired," he said gently. "Go to bed."
Stooping, he kissed her cheek. "Good-night. Happy
dreams."

unable to banish completely the little imp of per-

too-pleasant dreams, I mean."
She had hurt him!
Ann went to her room and
[58]prepared for bed. Why couldn't she have asked him

It was because he hadn't consulted her. She
opened the window, slipped into bed, turned off the
lamp on the bed-side table. That was an unjust

thought that she would be delighted as he was at

Their most magnificent Castle-in-Spain was built on

She hadn't expected it to happen. Grandmother

HE house was low and white with a shingled roof
THE
and blue shutters bolted across the windows. It

"Here's our house!" Ann cried, diminishing the
leisurely progress of the car.
"It is nice, isn't it?" Father agreed.
"Let's explore." Ann brought the modest road-

"It's sheer impertinence for you to drive so much
better than I do," Father complained, opening the

"Personality," Father said, stepping out into the

Ann opened the gate and walked toward the

"It's exactly right," she said, making a hasty sur-
vey of the house and grounds. "It's neither toolarge nor too small. There's a chimney, so there's
sure to be a fireplace. It's quiet-no close neigh-

"The season is later here." Father's voice indi-

pose there's a window without a shutter?"
They discovered, upon investigation, that all of
the windows were tightly barred. They discovered,
also, pleasant features not included in the first hasty
survey. There was a porch at the rear of the house,
thickly hung with vines.
"We'll eat there!" Ann decided, her enthusiasm
growing.
"Mosquitoes," Father suggested.
"Netting," she answered, "or screening, per-
haps."
The lawn, overgrown with weeds and dandelions,
sloped down from the porch to a retaining wall built

green, meeting in the distance the clear blue of the
sky.
"It's lovely!" Ann said a little breathlessly, stand-
ing at the top of the short flight of steps which led
down the beach. The air was clear and sparkling,
crisped with a salty tang. As she looked out across
the water, a gull rose from the surface of the bay,
wheeled up into the sky, its wings silver-white in the
sunshine. New York seemed very far away. She
was going to love the Cape. . .
"We must have it," she said. Her sweeping ges-
ture included the house and the weed-grown lawn,
[68]the apple trees, the bay and the sky, the diminish-
ing flash of gull wings. "We must have it, Father!"
"Yes," he said, "I think we must." He stood
leaning against the trunk of an apple tree, smoking,
smiling at her obvious delight. "How do we go
about acquiring such a treasure?"
"I don't know." Ann shook her head. "The sign
on the gate wasn't helpful."
"The Post-office, perhaps," Father said. "In a
town like this it's usually a bureau of information."
"Let's go!" she said impatiently. "Someone
may be there renting it now. You'll have to mow
this lawn, Jonathan. Ought we to eat the dande-
lions or make wine or something? We must be eco-
nomical."
Father laughed, tucked her hand under his arm.
"Let's dispose of the dandelions in one reckless ges-
ture," he said.
They walked around the house.
"These are climbing roses," Ann said, touching

wish we could see inside. I simply can't wait!
Father!" she said, stopping short on the flag-stone

we can afford?"
"Probably." He smiled at her anxiety. "You
look like Fifth Avenue." His smiling glance in-
cluded her sport-suit of gray angora, the trim gray
suede oxfords, the soft felt hat with its jaunty brim.
"That outfit is apt to be misleading."
"These clothes?" Ann asked, surprised. "They
weren't expensive."
Father, she thought, as they walked on to the car,
[ 69 ]had no idea how little of the money he had given

Most of it had gone to pay for the rosewood sofa.
Mr. MacArdle had agreed to keep it for her-in-
definitely. How much time would elapse before she
might claim her property? Where would she and
Father be living then? It didn't seem quite so im-
portant, now. And yet―
Quietly, her high spirits a little subdued, she turn-

"Don't worry," Father said, misunderstanding
her silence. "You can stay in the car. No one, I

"You!" She looked at him, smiling. "You look

"A dory," Father corrected.

They found the Post-office, a low frame building
